=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a grid with N rows and N columns, where N is an even number. Let (i, j) denote the cell at the i-th row from the top and j-th column from the left.
Each cell is painted black or white. If A_{i, j} = #, cell (i, j) is black; if A_{i, j} = ., it is white.
Find the color of each cell after performing the following operation for i = 1, 2, \ldots, \frac{N}{2} in this order.

- For all pairs of integers x, y between i and N + 1 - i, inclusive, replace the color of cell (y, N + 1 - x) with the color of cell (x, y). Perform these replacements simultaneously for all such pairs x, y.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
A_{1,1}A_{1,2}\ldots A_{1,N}
A_{2,1}A_{2,2}\ldots A_{2,N}
\vdots
A_{N,1}A_{N,2}\ldots A_{N,N}

Output

After all operations, let B_{i, j} = # if cell (i, j) is black, and B_{i, j} = . if it is white. Print the grid in the following format:
B_{1,1}B_{1,2}\ldots B_{1,N}
B_{2,1}B_{2,2}\ldots B_{2,N}
\vdots
B_{N,1}B_{N,2}\ldots B_{N,N}

Constraints


- N is an even number between 2 and 3000, inclusive.
- Each A_{i, j} is # or ..

Sample Input 1

8
.......#
.......#
.####..#
.####..#
.##....#
.##....#
.#######
.#######

Sample Output 1

........
#######.
#.....#.
#.###.#.
#.#...#.
#.#####.
#.......
########

The operations change the colors of the grid cells as follows:
.......#   ........   ........   ........   ........
.......#   ######..   #######.   #######.   #######.
.####..#   ######..   #....##.   #.....#.   #.....#.
.####..# → ##..##.. → #....##. → #.##..#. → #.###.#.
.##....#   ##..##..   #..####.   #.##..#.   #.#...#.
.##....#   ##......   #..####.   #.#####.   #.#####.
.#######   ##......   #.......   #.......   #.......
.#######   ########   ########   ########   ########

Sample Input 2

6
.#.#.#
##.#..
...###
###...
..#.##
#.#.#.

Sample Output 2

#.#.#.
.#.#.#
#.#.#.
.#.#.#
#.#.#.
.#.#.#

Sample Input 3

12
.......#.###
#...#...#..#
###.#..#####
..#.#.#.#...
.#.....#.###
.......#.#..
#...#..#....
#####.......
...#...#.#.#
..###..#..##
#..#.#.#.#.#
.####.......

Sample Output 3

.#..##...##.
#.#.#.#.#...
###.##..#...
#.#.#.#.#...
#.#.##...##.
............
............
.###.###.###
...#...#.#..
.###...#.###
...#...#...#
.###...#.###

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Let's verify T^2 = T(T(r,c)) = T(N+1-c, r) = (N+1-r, N+1-c). T^3 = T(N+1-r, N+1-c) = (N+1-(N+1-c), N+1-r) = (c, N+1-r). T^4=(r,c). Good.

N=3000, 9 million cells. Python loops 9 million with string indexing and bytearray assignment maybe okay ~3-5 sec. AtCoder? Need maybe optimize. import sys
def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    A = [input().strip() for _ in range(N)]
    out = []
    half = N // 2
    for r in range(1, N+1):
        row = bytearray(N)
        # maybe compute k for each c
        r2 = N + 1 - r
        # k = min(r, r2, c, N+1-c)
        # For fixed r, k depends on c and min(r,r2). Let a = min(r, r2). Then k = min(a, c, N+1-c). Since c and N+1-c min is distance to vertical border. Let b = min(c, N+1-c). k = min(a,b).
        a = r if r < r2 else r2
        for c in range(1, N+1):
            b = c if c < N+1-c else N+1-c
            k = a if a < b else b
            mod = k & 3? Since k%4. k & 3 works for positive int.
            if mod == 0:
                sr = r; sc = c
            elif mod == 1:
                sr = N + 1 - c; sc = r
            elif mod == 2:
                sr = N + 1 - r; sc = N + 1 - c
            else:
                sr = c; sc = N + 1 - r
            row[c-1] = A[sr-1][sc-1]
        out.append(bytes(row))
    sys.stdout.buffer.write(b'\n'.join(out))

Let's design ring approach in detail.

Given N even. For k from 1 to N/2:
L = N - 2*(k-1)  # side length of square k
M = 4*(L-1)
shift = (k * (L-1)) % M  # because k rotations, each rotation shifts by L-1 positions along ring sequence? Need verify for inner rings: Does f (CW rotation) shift ring sequence by L-1? For ring k, side length L. A 90° CW rotation maps top-left (k,k) to (k, N+1-k) = top-right. In clockwise sequence starting at top-left, top-right index L-1. So shift = L-1. Yes. After k rotations, shift = k*(L-1) mod M. Good.
Original ring sequence in clockwise order starting at top-left:
top side: (k, c) for c=k..N+1-k (left to right) length L.
right side: (r, N+1-k) for r=k+1..N-k (top+1 to bottom-1) length L-2.
bottom side: (N+1-k, c) for c=N+1-k down to k (right to left) length L.
left side: (r, k) for r=N-k down to k+1 (bottom-1 to top+1) length L-2.
Total 2L + 2L -4 = 4L-4 = M.

Final ring sequence = orig[-shift:] + orig[:-shift] if shift != 0. Since shift can be 0 (when k*(L-1) multiple of M). Then same.

Assign final ring to output positions in same clockwise order:
top side: B[k][c] = fin[pos] for c=k..N+1-k.
right side: B[r][N+1-k] = fin[pos] for r=k+1..N-k.
bottom side: B[N+1-k][c] = fin[pos] for c=N+1-k down to k.
left side: B[r][k] = fin[pos] for r=N-k down to k+1.

Need ensure output initialized? We fill all rings, covers all cells. Good.

Could we avoid storing fin separately by computing source index? But fin bytearray length M up to 12000, small. For each ring, create orig bytearray length M, fill. Then rotate. Then assign.

Memory: output rows bytearray N each. We can initialize out = [bytearray(N) for _ in range(N)] maybe 9MB. Then fill. At end join.

This matches final row1 m i e a. Good. Right side: B[2][4]=b, B[3][4]=c. Final row2 col4 b, row3 col4 c. Bottom: B[4][4]=d, B[4][3]=h, B[4][2]=l, B[4][1]=p. Final row4 p l h d? Wait final row4 from CW rotation of 4x4: p l h d? Let's compute final matrix:
m i e a
n j f b
o k g c
p l h d
Yes row4 p l h d. Our bottom assignment left-to-right? We assign bottom side in clockwise order starting at bottom-right? Wait clockwise sequence after top and right: bottom side should be traversed right-to-left. In final sequence fin positions after top (0..L-1), right (L..2L-3?), bottom starts at index 2L-2? Let's compute M=12, top length4 indices0-3: m,i,e,a. right length2 indices4-5: b,c. bottom length4 indices6-9: d,h,l,p. left length2 indices10-11: o,n. Assign bottom c from N+1-k down to k: c=4 -> d, c=3 -> h, c=2 -> l, c=1 -> p. So B[4] = [p,l,h,d]? Wait row4 col1 gets p at bottom last, col2 l, col3 h, col4 d. Yes row4 p l h d. Good.

Left side: indices10-11: o,n assigned r=N-k=3 down to k+1=2: r=3 col1 o, r=2 col1 n. Final row3 col1 o, row2 col1 n. Good.

So ring approach works.

Now, is shift = k*(L-1) mod M always? Let's test inner ring k=2 for N=4: L=2, M=4, shift=2*(1)=2 mod4=2. Original central ring sequence: (2,2)=f,(2,3)=g,(3,3)=k,(3,2)=j. After op1 whole CW, central ring becomes? Original 4x4 CW:

Then op2 rotates central 2x2 CW: matrix central [[j,f],[k,g]] CW -> [[k,j],[g,f]]? Actually CW of 2x2 [[a,b],[c,d]] -> [[c,a],[d,b]] = [[k,j],[g,f]]. So final central: row2 col2 k, row2 col3 j, row3 col3 f, row3 col2 g. Sequence clockwise from top-left: k,j,f,g. Original central sequence [f,g,k,j]? Let's list original central clockwise: (2,2)=f,(2,3)=g,(3,3)=k,(3,2)=j. Final [k,j,f,g] = orig[-2:]+orig[:-2] = [k,j,f,g]. shift=2. Good.

Thus ring approach is correct.

Which implementation is simpler and faster? Direct per-cell formula is conceptually simple and less code. Ring approach maybe more code but avoids min/mod per cell and uses slicing for horizontal. Need decide.

Let's consider performance of ring approach in Python.

Pseudo:

def main():
    import sys
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    A = [input().strip() for _ in range(N)]
    B = [bytearray(N) for _ in range(N)]
    half = N // 2
    for k in range(1, half+1):
        L = N - 2*(k-1)
        M = 4*(L-1)
        shift = (k * (L-1)) % M
        orig = bytearray(M)
        pos = 0
        top = k-1
        bottom = N-k
        left = k-1
        right = N-k
        # top side
        row = A[top]
        orig[pos:pos+L] = row[left:right+1]  # bytes slice
        pos += L
        # right side
        for r in range(top+1, bottom):
            orig[pos] = A[r][right]
            pos += 1
        # bottom side
        row = A[bottom]
        # from right to left
        orig[pos:pos+L] = row[left:right+1][::-1]  # bytes reversed slice
        pos += L
        # left side
        for r in range(bottom-1, top, -1):
            orig[pos] = A[r][left]
            pos += 1
        # rotate
        if shift:
            shift2 = M - shift
            fin = orig[shift2:] + orig[:shift2]  # bytearray? bytes? orig[shift2:] is bytearray, + bytearray -> bytearray
        else:
            fin = orig
        pos = 0
        # assign top
        outrow = B[top]
        outrow[left:right+1] = fin[pos:pos+L]
        pos += L
        # right side
        for r in range(top+1, bottom):
            B[r][right] = fin[pos]
            pos += 1
        # bottom
        outrow = B[bottom]
        outrow[left:right+1] = fin[pos:pos+L][::-1]? Wait fin bottom segment is in clockwise order right-to-left. We need assign to row slice left:right+1. If fin[pos] corresponds to bottom-right, fin[pos+L-1] bottom-left. To assign row[left:right+1] = [bottom-right, ..., bottom-left], we need reverse the segment. So outrow[left:right+1] = fin[pos:pos+L][::-1].
        pos += L
        # left side
        for r in range(bottom-1, top, -1):
            B[r][left] = fin[pos]
            pos += 1

Need check slice assignment with bytearray and bytes reversed. `row[left:right+1][::-1]` creates bytes? If row is bytes, slicing returns bytes, reversed returns bytes. Good. `orig[pos:pos+L] = row[left:right+1]` works? bytearray slice assignment accepts bytes-like. Yes.

Potential issue: `orig[pos:pos+L] = row[left:right+1]` if lengths match. Yes.

`fin = orig[shift2:] + orig[:shift2]` if orig is bytearray, slicing returns bytearray, concatenation returns bytearray. If shift=0, fin=orig. Good. But if shift !=0, fin is new bytearray length M. If shift=0, fin references orig; okay.

Then assign top slice: `outrow[left:right+1] = fin[pos:pos+L]` works if fin is bytearray. For bottom, `fin[pos:pos+L][::-1]` returns bytearray reversed. Good.

Need ensure B rows initialized with zeros; all cells filled exactly once. Yes.

Let's test ring approach with sample 1 maybe? Could manually small N=2:

We used that. Good.

Direct formula code:

import sys
def main():
    input=sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N=int(input())
    A=[input().strip() for _ in range(N)]
    out=[]
    for r in range(1,N+1):
        row=bytearray(N)
        a = r if r < N+1-r else N+1-r
        r2 = N+1-r
        for c in range(1,N+1):
            b = c if c < N+1-c else N+1-c
            k = a if a < b else b
            m = k & 3
            if m == 0:
                sr=r; sc=c
            elif m == 1:
                sr=N+1-c; sc=r
            elif m == 2:
                sr=r2; sc=N+1-c
            else:
                sr=c; sc=r2
            row[c-1] = A[sr-1][sc-1]
        out.append(bytes(row))
    sys.stdout.buffer.write(b'\n'.join(out))

This is simple. But inner loop has Python if/elif and min. 9e6 iterations. Might be okay. But maybe time limit tight? AtCoder typical 2 sec? N=3000, Python 9e6 loops with branches maybe ~3-4 sec. Let's refine ring code for speed and clarity.

```
import sys

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    A = [input().strip() for _ in range(N)]
    B = [bytearray(N) for _ in range(N)]

    half = N // 2
    for k in range(1, half + 1):
        top = k - 1
        bottom = N - k
        left = k - 1
        right = N - k
        L = right - left + 1  # N - 2*(k-1)
        M = 4 * (L - 1)
        shift = (k & 3) * (L - 1)
        # If shift == M? shift max 3(L-1) < M for L>1, so shift < M.
        orig = bytearray(M)
        pos = 0

        # top side
        row = A[top]
        orig[pos:pos+L] = row[left:right+1]
        pos += L

        # right side
        for r in range(top + 1, bottom):
            orig[pos] = A[r][right]
            pos += 1

        # bottom side
        row = A[bottom]
        orig[pos:pos+L] = row[left:right+1][::-1]
        pos += L

        # left side
        for r in range(bottom - 1, top, -1):
            orig[pos] = A[r][left]
            pos += 1

        # rotate
        if shift:
            cut = M - shift
            fin = orig[cut:] + orig[:cut]
        else:
            fin = orig

        pos = 0
        # top side
        outrow = B[top]
        outrow[left:right+1] = fin[pos:pos+L]
        pos += L

        # right side
        for r in range(top + 1, bottom):
            B[r][right] = fin[pos]
            pos += 1

        # bottom side
        outrow = B[bottom]
        outrow[left:right+1] = fin[pos:pos+L][::-1]
        pos += L

        # left side
        for r in range(bottom - 1, top, -1):
            B[r][left] = fin[pos]
            pos += 1

    sys.stdout.buffer.write(b'\n'.join(B))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Need check `shift = (k & 3) * (L - 1)`. If k=4, shift=0. If shift=0, fin=orig. If shift nonzero, cut = M - shift. Since shift < M, cut in 1..M-1. Good.

Potential issue: `orig[pos:pos+L] = row[left:right+1]` for top and bottom: `row` is bytes. But `row[left:right+1]` creates a copy. For bottom, `row[left:right+1][::-1]` creates another. Fine. Could use `row[right:left:-1]`? For bytes, `row[right:left:-1]` gives from right down to left+1, missing left. Need include left: `row[right:left-1:-1]` if left>0; but if left=0, `row[right:-1:-1]`? Actually `row[right:left-1:-1]` with left=0 gives row[right:-1:-1] which excludes index0? Let's not risk; use [::-1].

Potential issue: `outrow[left:right+1] = fin[pos:pos+L][::-1]` if fin is bytearray, `fin[pos:pos+L][::-1]` returns bytearray reversed. Slice assignment accepts. Good.

Potential issue: `b'\n'.join(B)` where B contains bytearray. Let's confirm. If uncertain, use:
```
    sys.stdout.buffer.write(b'\n'.join(B))
```
I know it works. But to be absolutely safe, we can convert B rows to bytes before join:
```
    sys.stdout.buffer.write(b'\n'.join(bytes(row) for row in B))
```
This creates 3000 bytes objects and a 9MB joined bytes. Memory still okay. But generator passed to join will be consumed; join first converts to sequence? It may build list internally? It accepts any iterable, but will create a list? It likely does PySequence_Fast, so list of bytes. Fine. Extra memory ~9MB. Still okay. But `b'\n'.join(B)` avoids extra bytes objects? It still creates output bytes. It accepts bytearray. Let's use `b'\n'.join(B)` for efficiency. I'm confident.

Let's verify bytes.join with bytearray in Python 3. From docs: "join(iterable_of_bytes)" Return a bytes object which is the concatenation of the bytes objects in iterable_of_bytes. "The separator between elements is the contents of the bytes or bytearray object providing this method." It doesn't explicitly say elements must be bytes, but "bytes objects" maybe. However, CPython's bytes.join accepts any object that supports buffer protocol. Example: `b' '.join([bytearray(b'a')])` returns b'a'. I've used it. So okay.

Now, let's think if `input().strip()` on a row of all dots could strip dots? No, `strip()` removes whitespace, not dots. Good.

Now, let's consider if N=3000, recursion no. Time: The ring approach uses `orig[pos:pos+L] = row[left:right+1]` for top/bottom. But `row[left:right+1]` for bytes returns a new bytes object. For bottom, `[::-1]` returns another. For each ring, top/bottom slices total 4L bytes copied into orig/out. Total ~18MB C-level copies. Fine.

Could we avoid creating `fin` when shift=0? We do. If shift !=0, `fin = orig[cut:] + orig[:cut]` creates two bytearray slices and concatenates. Total M bytes. Fine.

Potential issue: `orig[cut:] + orig[:cut]` when orig is bytearray: slicing returns bytearray, `+` returns bytearray. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the final assignment's left side: We assign r from bottom-1 to top+1. fin segment is bottom-to-top. Good.

Now, let's think if there is any issue with the final assignment's right side: We assign r from top+1 to bottom-1. fin segment is top-to-bottom. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the final assignment's top side: We assign slice left-to-right. fin segment is left-to-right. Good.

Now, let's think if there is any issue with the gather's right side: We append A[r][right] for r top+1..bottom-1. That's top-to-bottom. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the gather's left side: We append A[r][left] for r bottom-1..top+1. That's bottom-to-top. Good.

Now, let's think if there is any issue with the gather's bottom side: We append row[left:right+1][::-1]. That's right-to-left. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the gather's top side: We append row[left:right+1]. That's left-to-right. Good.

Now, let's think if there is any issue with the ring sequence orientation: We chose clockwise starting at top-left. The operation f is clockwise rotation, so top-left -> top-right. In clockwise sequence, top-right is after top side, index L-1. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the shift sign if f is clockwise but sequence is clockwise. We used final = O[-shift:]+O[:-shift]. Let's re-derive with sequence indexing one more time:
Let O[i] be original color at sequence position i. After one CW rotation, the cell at sequence position i moves to sequence position i+shift (mod M). So the color at final position j is the color that was at position j-shift. Thus F[j] = O[(j-shift) mod M]. This is exactly O[-shift:]+O[:-shift]. Example shift=1: F[0]=O[-1], F[1]=O[0], etc. So final sequence is original rotated right by 1. Good.

Now, let's think if there is any issue with the fact that operation i rotates the square, and the ring sequence position shift is L_k-1, but after multiple operations, the shift accumulates. Since f is same, yes.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the ring k being affected by operation i for i<k, but operation i's square side L_i is larger. Does f on ring k shift by L_k-1 or by L_i-1? We already know f is geometric 90° rotation, so top-left of ring k maps to top-right of ring k, index L_k-1. It doesn't depend on L_i. Let's verify with coordinates for N=6 ring2: top-left (2,2) maps to (2,5). Ring2 top side length 4, top-right index3. L_k-1=3. If we used L_i-1 for op1 (L_1=6, shift5), that would be wrong. Our formula uses L_k. Good.

Now, let's think if there is any issue with the operation i for i<k rotating the entire square, and the ring k's top-left after rotation maps to top-right of ring k, but is top-right of ring k at index L_k-1 in the ring sequence? Yes, because ring sequence top side from left to right includes all cells row k, cols k..N+1-k. Top-right is the last cell of top side, index L_k-1. Good.

=== ANSWER ===
with code.</think>```python
import sys

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    A = [input().strip() for _ in range(N)]

    B = [bytearray(N) for _ in range(N)]

    for k in range(1, N // 2 + 1):
        top = k - 1
        bottom = N - k
        left = k - 1
        right = N - k

        L = right - left + 1
        M = 4 * (L - 1)
        shift = (k & 3) * (L - 1)

        orig = bytearray(M)
        pos = 0

        # top side: left -> right
        row = A[top]
        orig[pos:pos + L] = row[left:right + 1]
        pos += L

        # right side: top -> bottom
        for r in range(top + 1, bottom):
            orig[pos] = A[r][right]
            pos += 1

        # bottom side: right -> left
        row = A[bottom]
        orig[pos:pos + L] = row[left:right + 1][::-1]
        pos += L

        # left side: bottom -> top
        for r in range(bottom - 1, top, -1):
            orig[pos] = A[r][left]
            pos += 1

        if shift:
            cut = M - shift
            fin = orig[cut:] + orig[:cut]
        else:
            fin = orig

        pos = 0

        # top side
        outrow = B[top]
        outrow[left:right + 1] = fin[pos:pos + L]
        pos += L

        # right side
        for r in range(top + 1, bottom):
            B[r][right] = fin[pos]
            pos += 1

        # bottom side
        outrow = B[bottom]
        outrow[left:right + 1] = fin[pos:pos + L][::-1]
        pos += L

        # left side
        for r in range(bottom - 1, top, -1):
            B[r][left] = fin[pos]
            pos += 1

    out = sys.stdout.buffer
    for i, row in enumerate(B):
        if i:
            out.write(b'\n')
        out.write(row)
    out.write(b'\n')

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
